- **Abstract**: With the rapid advancement of large language models (LLMs), reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as a pivotal methodology for enhancing the reasoning capabilities of LLMs. Unlike traditional pre-training approaches, RL encompasses multiple stages: rollout, reward, and training, which necessitates collaboration among various worker types. However, current RL systems continue to grapple with substantial GPU underutilization, due to two primary factors: (1) The rollout stage dominates the overall RL process due to test-time scaling; (2) Imbalances in rollout lengths (within the same batch) result in GPU bubbles. While prior solutions like asynchronous execution and truncation offer partial relief, they may compromise training accuracy for efficiency. Our key insight stems from a previously overlooked observation: rollout responses exhibit remarkable similarity across adjacent training epochs. Based on the insight, we introduce RhymeRL, an LLM RL system designed to accelerate RL training with two key innovations. First, to enhance rollout generation, we present HistoSpec, a speculative decoding inference engine that utilizes the similarity of historical rollout token sequences to obtain accurate drafts. Second, to tackle rollout bubbles, we introduce HistoPipe, a two-tier scheduling strategy that leverages the similarity of historical rollout distributions to balance workload among rollout workers. We have evaluated RhymeRL within a real production environment, demonstrating scalability from dozens to thousands of GPUs. Experimental results demonstrate that RhymeRL achieves a 2.6x performance improvement over existing methods, without compromising accuracy or modifying the RL paradigm.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "History Rhymes: Accelerating LLM Reinforcement Learning with RhymeRL" addresses the significant GPU underutilization issues in reinforcement learning (RL) training of large language models (LLMs).  The authors identify two main bottlenecks: the dominance of the rollout stage (due to test-time scaling) and GPU bubbles caused by imbalances in rollout lengths. To overcome these, the paper proposes RhymeRL, an LLM RL system with two innovations:

1.  **HistoSpec:** A speculative decoding engine leveraging historical rollout token sequence similarity to generate accurate drafts, speeding up rollout. It makes use of a suffix tree based approach for efficient draft generation.
2.  **HistoPipe:** A two-tier scheduling strategy utilizing historical rollout distribution similarity to balance workload among rollout workers, minimizing GPU bubbles. HistoPipe is a distribution-aware hybrid pipeline that uses historical distribution information.

The authors evaluated RhymeRL in a production environment, demonstrating a 2.6x performance improvement over existing methods without compromising accuracy or altering the RL paradigm.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core ideas, HistoSpec and HistoPipe, offer incremental but valuable contributions. The use of historical rollout data for speculative decoding (HistoSpec) is an interesting approach to optimize the rollout phase, distinguishing it from traditional LLM inference. The use of a suffix tree to efficiently query historical data, combined with a TCP congestion control inspired approach to adjust the length of the speculation draft is a strong and novel method to apply to LLM training. HistoPipe, the distribution-aware scheduling strategy, is another significant contribution to reduce GPU underutilization that utilizes historical distribution data. Both, however, are more in the realm of system-level optimizations for RL specifically for LLMs than breakthroughs in the RL algorithms themselves, with the algorithm remaining largely untouched. The approach uses speculative decoding, an existing technique, but applies it uniquely in the RL training context and combines it with novel components (reward awareness, suffix tree indexing, congestion control inspired speculation window, two tier scheduling). This context-aware and carefully constructed application differentiates RhymeRL from other speculative decoding works.

*   **Significance:** The paper tackles a crucial practical problem in LLM RL: the inefficient use of expensive GPU resources. Addressing these bottlenecks directly impacts the cost and scalability of training high-performing LLMs with RL. The experimental results, demonstrating a 2.6x speedup in a real-world production environment, are compelling and showcase the potential for substantial resource savings. The fact that the approach is implemented on top of existing frameworks like veRL, and is compatible with other algorithms (GRPO, DAPO), increases its practical significance and ease of adoption. Given the dominance of the rollout phase in LLM training, anything that accelerates it has a major impact.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practical Focus:** The paper addresses real-world bottlenecks and proposes solutions that are deployable in production settings.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The analysis of GPU underutilization and its sources is well-articulated.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The performance improvements are substantial and well-supported by experiments on real-world datasets and varying model sizes.
    *   **System-Level Optimizations:** Provides effective techniques to integrate with existing LLM RL frameworks, focusing on system level considerations.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the combination of techniques and their application to LLM RL is novel, the individual components (speculative decoding, basic scheduling) are not entirely groundbreaking.
    *   **Limited Algorithmic Impact:** The paper primarily focuses on system optimization rather than proposing new RL algorithms.
    *   **Dependency on Historical Data:** The system relies on stable model evolution and historical data availability. While the authors argue that clipping addresses this, there could be scenarios (e.g., catastrophic forgetting, extreme model updates) where the historical information is less reliable.

*   **Potential Influence:** RhymeRL has the potential to significantly impact the practical training of LLMs with RL. Its adoption could lead to reduced training costs, faster iteration cycles, and increased accessibility to RL-based LLM training for organizations with limited resources. The detailed characterization of RL in the wild is also useful. The insights regarding token similarity, length distributions, and historical data relevance are contributions in themselves.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

RhymeRL is a strong paper that presents a practically significant solution to a critical problem in LLM RL. It demonstrates substantial performance improvements in a real-world setting, and the proposed techniques are well-engineered and compatible with existing frameworks. Although the core ideas rely on incremental improvements to established methods, their integration and application within the specific context of LLM RL are novel and valuable. The system-level optimizations and detailed characterization of real world LLM RL workflows make this an important contribution, and the thorough ablation studies and analysis enhance confidence in its performance and broader impact on the field.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### SemLayoutDiff: Semantic Layout Generation with Diffusion Model for Indoor Scene Synthesis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.18597v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaohao Sun, Divyam Goel, Angle X. Chang
- **Abstract**: We present SemLayoutDiff, a unified model for synthesizing diverse 3D indoor scenes across multiple room types. The model introduces a scene layout representation combining a top-down semantic map and attributes for each object. Unlike prior approaches, which cannot condition on architectural constraints, SemLayoutDiff employs a categorical diffusion model capable of conditioning scene synthesis explicitly on room masks. It first generates a coherent semantic map, followed by a cross-attention-based network to predict furniture placements that respect the synthesized layout. Our method also accounts for architectural elements such as doors and windows, ensuring that generated furniture arrangements remain practical and unobstructed. Experiments on the 3D-FRONT dataset show that SemLayoutDiff produces spatially coherent, realistic, and varied scenes, outperforming previous methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SemLayoutDiff, a diffusion-based model for generating 3D indoor scenes. It addresses limitations in existing methods, specifically: the lack of architectural awareness, object interpenetration, and unified model training for different room types. SemLayoutDiff uses a 2D semantic layout map to represent the scene, which includes architectural elements like floors, doors, and windows, enabling the model to generate rooms and furniture arrangements simultaneously. The model employs a categorical diffusion process to generate the semantic map and a cross-attention network to predict furniture placement respecting the generated layout. It demonstrates improved spatial coherence, realism, and variety compared to prior methods on the 3D-FRONT dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The use of a 2D semantic layout map that explicitly includes architectural elements *is* a useful representation that aids in scene generation. Prior work often treated these elements as separate conditions or did not handle them at all.

    *   Using a *categorical diffusion model* to directly generate the semantic map is a reasonable approach, given the discrete nature of the data. Compared to existing diffusion model, this paper's usage is novel by unified handling various architectures and conditioning on room masks.

    *   The cross-attention network for predicting furniture attributes based on the semantic map offers a plausible mechanism for respecting the overall layout.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper addresses a relevant and practical problem in computer graphics and AI: automated scene generation.

    *   The emphasis on architectural coherence *is* important for creating realistic and usable scenes, especially in AR/VR applications or for training embodied agents.

    *   The unified model approach that can handle different room types is also beneficial, reducing the need for separate training for each room.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and well-articulated limitations of existing methods.
    *   Effective use of a 2D semantic layout representation.
    *   Demonstrated improvements in spatial coherence and plausibility.
    *   Unified approach for different room types.
    *   Qualitative and quantitative results support the claims.
    *   Detailed ablation studies.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Reliance on the 3D-FRONT dataset, which has limitations in terms of scene diversity and object categories. The model's ability to generalize to more diverse and complex scenes is not fully explored.

    *   The attribute prediction and object retrieval step is not fully addressed. The authors acknowledge limitations with object shapes or contextual information.

    *   Vertical relationships and occlusion are not well-handled due to the 2D representation.

    *   Some of the comparisons are limited. For example, failing to find comparable code for related works and compare the performances.

*   **Potential Influence:** The work provides a practical approach to indoor scene generation that addresses some key limitations of existing methods. It's potential lies in applications such as AR/VR content creation and training data generation for AI.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper makes a *solid* contribution to indoor scene generation by introducing a coherent representation that captures architectural context and furniture relationships, and provides a unified diffusion based model to handle complex layouts. This offers a pragmatic approach, addressing some critical limitations of prior art. The experiments demonstrated advantages in terms of generating plausible and spatially coherent scenes. That said, the reliance on the 3D-FRONT dataset and the limitations of the 2D representation limit the scope of the work, and the attribute prediction / object retrieval aspects require further refinement, impacting the overall level of breakthrough innovation. While the results are promising, a score above 7 would require more significant advances to solve those limits, for example generalization to more diverse data or extending the approach to better handle vertical relationships.

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 7/10

### What do language models model? Transformers, automata, and the format of thought
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.18598v1)
- **Authors**: Colin Klein
- **Abstract**: What do large language models actually model? Do they tell us something about human capacities, or are they models of the corpus we've trained them on? I give a non-deflationary defence of the latter position. Cognitive science tells us that linguistic capabilities in humans rely supralinear formats for computation. The transformer architecture, by contrast, supports at best a linear formats for processing. This argument will rely primarily on certain invariants of the computational architecture of transformers. I then suggest a positive story about what transformers are doing, focusing on Liu et al. (2022)'s intriguing speculations about shortcut automata. I conclude with why I don't think this is a terribly deflationary story. Language is not (just) a means for expressing inner state but also a kind of 'discourse machine' that lets us make new language given appropriate context. We have learned to use this technology in one way; LLMs have also learned to use it too, but via very different means.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "What Do Language Models Model? Transformers, Automata, and the Format of Thought," based on the provided text:

**Summary:**

The paper explores what Large Language Models (LLMs) actually model: human linguistic capacity or the training corpus itself.  The author argues against LLMs directly modeling human cognitive capacities, positing that human linguistic competence relies on "supralinear" computational formats, whereas transformers (specifically encoder-only autoregressive ones) operate primarily with linear formats (or, at best, derived formats built upon a sublinear base).  The argument centers on two invariants of the transformer architecture: permutation invariance (in unmasked transformers) and substring invariance (in masked transformers).  The author then offers a positive account: LLMs model the input-to-state function of a corpus-producing finite-state automaton (FSA), emulating the automaton's behaviour.  This approach leverages the Krohn-Rhodes theorem. The author concludes by arguing that LLMs provide insight into the nature of corpora as 'discourse machines' and not necessarily as models of human cognition.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's attempt to tie transformer capabilities to the computational constraints imposed by their architectural invariants (permutation/substring invariance) and to link them to shortcut solutions for emulating finite-state automata is novel.  The use of the Krohn-Rhodes theorem, while not entirely new in this context (Liu et al., 2022, are cited), is leveraged for a specific philosophical argument about the limitations and potentials of LLMs. The explicit comparison to human cognitive architecture and the emphasis on computational format is also a distinctive feature.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a central and increasingly debated question in the field: what are LLMs *really* doing? The claim that LLMs primarily model the corpus, as opposed to human cognition, aligns with some existing deflationary perspectives but attempts to offer a more nuanced explanation. The argument regarding the linear vs. supralinear format is a significant attempt to ground this debate in concrete computational constraints, adding a new dimension to the discussion. The idea that LLMs are shortcut emulators of finite-state automata is a potentially fruitful direction for understanding their behavior and limitations.

**Strengths:**
*   **Clear and well-structured argument:** The paper proceeds logically, building from the premise of computational formats to architectural invariants and finally to the automata emulation theory.
*   **Engagement with cognitive science literature:** The argument is grounded in cognitive science principles and addresses existing debates about the language of thought and the nature of representation.
*   **Concrete focus on mathematical invariants:** The use of permutation and substring invariance provides a specific, testable (in principle) basis for the argument. This distinguishes it from more hand-wavy critiques of LLMs.
*   **Positive account offered:** The paper doesn't merely criticize LLMs; it proposes an alternative explanation of their capabilities.
*   **Nuanced conclusion:** The author avoids a purely deflationary view by emphasizing that the corpus itself has structure and that LLMs can reveal something about that structure.

**Weaknesses:**
*   **Reliance on simple LLMs:** The focus on simple encoder-only transformers limits the generalizability of the argument to more complex architectures (e.g., decoder-only or encoder-decoder). The field is rapidly progressing and generalizations from basic architecture to more complex architectures need to be carefully done.
*   **Idealized representation of human cognition:** The characterization of human linguistic competence as relying on supralinear formats could be oversimplified.
*   **Speculative nature of the automata emulation theory:** While intriguing, the suggestion that LLMs are emulating finite-state automata is largely speculative and requires further empirical investigation. The justification based on Krohn-Rhodes is somewhat abstract and doesn't provide detailed mechanisms.
*   **Limited empirical evidence:** The paper is primarily theoretical and relies on abstract arguments rather than extensive empirical analysis of LLM behavior.
*   **Definition of format requires more clarity:** The definition of format, while useful for the argument, could benefit from more formalization and defense against alternative interpretations.

**Score:** 7/10

**Rationale:** The paper demonstrates significant novelty in its computational format driven account of the limitations and abilities of LLMs, and it is highly significant by connecting the LLM debates with classic questions in cognitive science and automata theory. It offers a well-reasoned argument grounded in specific architectural invariants. *However*, the speculative nature of the automata emulation theory, the focus on very simple LLMs, and the limited empirical support weaken the argument. While the paper provides a valuable contribution to the debate on what LLMs model, more concrete evidence and further investigation of the proposed mechanisms are needed to fully assess its impact. The author explicitly acknowledges some of these limitations (and archiving the unfinished work), which are key to its lower ranking. The paper's influence will depend on whether it inspires further research and provides a fruitful framework for understanding the inner workings of LLMs, but it's very specific approach to the problem is a major strength of the analysis.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Bias-Adjusted LLM Agents for Human-Like Decision-Making via Behavioral Economics
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.18600v1)
- **Authors**: Ayato Kitadai, Yusuke Fukasawa, Nariaki Nishino
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to simulate human decision-making, but their intrinsic biases often diverge from real human behavior--limiting their ability to reflect population-level diversity. We address this challenge with a persona-based approach that leverages individual-level behavioral data from behavioral economics to adjust model biases. Applying this method to the ultimatum game--a standard but difficult benchmark for LLMs--we observe improved alignment between simulated and empirical behavior, particularly on the responder side. While further refinement of trait representations is needed, our results demonstrate the promise of persona-conditioned LLMs for simulating human-like decision patterns at scale.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Bias-Adjusted LLM Agents for Human-Like Decision-Making via Behavioral Economics":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of biases in Large Language Models (LLMs) when used to simulate human decision-making.  The authors propose a persona-based approach to adjust LLM biases by leveraging individual-level behavioral data from behavioral economics, specifically the Econographics dataset.  They inject behavioral traits as personas into LLMs and evaluate the method on the ultimatum game, observing improved alignment between simulated and empirical behavior, especially on the responder side. They find that persona-conditioned LLMs can more closely mimic human-like decision patterns at scale.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea of using behavioral economics traits as personas to adjust LLM biases for more realistic decision-making simulation has some novelty. While other works have attempted to create human-like LLM agents, this approach specifically utilizes the Econographics dataset to reflect individual-level variations in behavioral indicators. This is a relatively new application of behavioral economics data to LLM agent modeling.
* **Significance:** Improving the human-likeness of LLM agents is important for various applications, including policy simulations and virtual experiments. The ultimatum game serves as a good benchmark for evaluating fairness and social preferences, which are often lacking in standard LLMs. The improved alignment achieved on the responder side suggests the potential of this method to better reflect human diversity in decision-making.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly identifies the problem of biases in LLM-based simulations of human behavior.
    * **Methodological Rigor:** The experiments are well-defined, using established datasets and benchmarks (Econographics, Ultimatum game data). The comparison between different persona settings provides insights into the impact of behavioral traits.
    * **Quantitative Evaluation:** The use of Wasserstein distance to compare simulated and empirical distributions offers a quantitative measure of alignment.
    * **Analysis of Results:** The paper provides a detailed analysis of the results, highlighting model-specific effects and potential explanations for the observed improvements and limitations.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope:** The evaluation is limited to the ultimatum game. The generalizability of the findings to other decision-making scenarios or economic games is not fully explored.
    * **Proposer Side Limitations:** The improvements on the proposer side are less significant, suggesting that the current persona representation may not fully capture the complexities of strategic reasoning.
    * **Model-Specific Effects:**  The paper notes that the effectiveness of persona conditioning depends on the specific LLM architecture. The need to tailor persona design to each LLM could be a limitation in practice. The explanation on why certain models work better under certain trait configurations is not clearly supported by the empirical results.
    * **Lack of Fine-tuning Comparison:** The paper acknowledges the lack of comparison with fine-tuning. It is unclear if the performance would be better by fine-tuning on similar tasks.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a promising approach for adjusting LLM biases in decision-making simulations. The use of Econographics data to create individual personas is a novel and potentially impactful technique. While the results are encouraging, the limitations related to scope, proposer behavior, and model-specific effects suggest that further research is needed to fully realize the potential of this method. The paper would be stronger if the reasons certain models perform better with specific trait configurations were further explored.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:**

The paper introduces a novel idea and demonstrates its potential effectiveness in a specific context (ultimatum game). However, the limitations related to generalizability, proposer behavior, and model-specific dependencies prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution. The lack of comparison against fine-tuning methods also reduces the perceived novelty. It's a solid first step that warrants further exploration, but doesn't represent a major leap forward in the field. The impact on the field will depend on how the presented work can be expanded to better decision-making tasks.

- **Classification**: cs.GT
- **Score**: 6/10

### Scaling Laws for Task-Stratified Knowledge in Post-Training Quantized Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.18609v1)
- **Authors**: Chenxi Zhou, Pengfei Cao, Jiang Li, Jun Zhao, Kang Liu
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) present significant deployment challenges due to their scale, with post-training quantization (PTQ) emerging as a practical compression solution. However, a comprehensive understanding of how PTQ precisely impacts diverse LLM knowledge capabilities remains elusive, and existing scaling laws for quantized models often overlook crucial PTQ-specific parameters and task-specific sensitivities. This paper addresses these gaps by conducting an extensive empirical investigation to establish task-stratified scaling laws. We disentangle LLM knowledge into memorization and utilization capabilities and develop a unified quantitative framework that incorporates model size, effective bit-width, calibration set size, and group size. Our central finding reveals that knowledge memorization exhibits markedly greater sensitivity to variations in effective bit-width, calibration set size, and model size compared to the more robust knowledge utilization. These findings offer a fine-grained understanding of PTQ's impact and provide guidance for developing knowledge-aware quantization strategies that can better preserve targeted cognitive functions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper addresses the limited understanding of how post-training quantization (PTQ) impacts the knowledge capabilities of large language models (LLMs). It introduces task-stratified scaling laws that disentangle LLM knowledge into memorization and utilization capabilities.  The study systematically investigates the impact of model size, effective bit-width, calibration set size, and group size on these two types of knowledge.  The central finding is that knowledge memorization is significantly more sensitive to variations in bit-width, calibration set size, and model size compared to knowledge utilization. The paper offers a framework for knowledge-aware quantization strategies. The authors test on OPT and LLaMa2 model families.

**Critical Evaluation**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its task-stratified approach to understanding the impact of quantization on LLM knowledge capabilities. While scaling laws for quantized models exist, the focus on disentangling memorization and utilization is a valuable contribution. The systematic incorporation of PTQ-specific parameters like calibration set size and group size is also novel and addresses a "granularity gap" in existing research.

**Significance:** The paper's findings have significant practical implications. Understanding the different sensitivities of knowledge memorization and utilization to quantization parameters can guide the development of more effective knowledge-aware PTQ strategies. This, in turn, can improve the deployment of quantized LLMs while preserving targeted cognitive functions. The analysis of key variables model size, bit-width, calibration set size, and group size is also useful.

**Strengths:**

*   **Task-Stratified Approach:** Distinguishing between knowledge memorization and utilization provides a more nuanced understanding of quantization's effects.
*   **Comprehensive Parameterization:** Incorporating PTQ-specific parameters like calibration set size and group size is a significant improvement over existing scaling law research that often overlooks these factors.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The study uses a large number of PTQ configurations to empirically validate its scaling laws.
*   **Practical Implications:** The findings offer actionable insights for developing better PTQ strategies.
*   **Clear Writing and Structure:** The paper is well-written and clearly structured.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Quantization Methods:** The study focuses primarily on GPTQ which may not be applicable to other techniques.  While they test on OPT and LLaMA2, other architectures would increase robustness.
*   **Simplistic Knowledge Stratification:** The memorization/utilization dichotomy, while useful, is still a simplification of the complex cognitive functions of LLMs. More fine-grained task classifications could yield further insights.
*   **Specific Task Selection:** While the tasks selected for KM/KU are standard, justification as to why each task was selected would be more robust.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by providing a more fine-grained understanding of how quantization affects LLMs. The task-stratified approach and comprehensive parameterization are significant strengths. The limitations regarding the scope of quantization methods and simplistic knowledge stratification prevent it from achieving a higher score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### RLMR: Reinforcement Learning with Mixed Rewards for Creative Writing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.18642v1)
- **Authors**: Jianxing Liao, Tian Zhang, Xiao Feng, Yusong Zhang, Rui Yang, Haorui Wang, Bosi Wen, Ziying Wang, Runzhi Shi
- **Abstract**: Large language models are extensively utilized in creative writing applications. Creative writing requires a balance between subjective writing quality (e.g., literariness and emotional expression) and objective constraint following (e.g., format requirements and word limits). Existing reinforcement learning methods struggle to balance these two aspects: single reward strategies fail to improve both abilities simultaneously, while fixed-weight mixed-reward methods lack the ability to adapt to different writing scenarios. To address this problem, we propose Reinforcement Learning with Mixed Rewards (RLMR), utilizing a dynamically mixed reward system from a writing reward model evaluating subjective writing quality and a constraint verification model assessing objective constraint following. The constraint following reward weight is adjusted dynamically according to the writing quality within sampled groups, ensuring that samples violating constraints get negative advantage in GRPO and thus penalized during training, which is the key innovation of this proposed method. We conduct automated and manual evaluations across diverse model families from 8B to 72B parameters. Additionally, we construct a real-world writing benchmark named WriteEval for comprehensive evaluation. Results illustrate that our method achieves consistent improvements in both instruction following (IFEval from 83.36\% to 86.65\%) and writing quality (72.75\% win rate in manual expert pairwise evaluations on WriteEval). To the best of our knowledge, RLMR is the first work to combine subjective preferences with objective verification in online RL training, providing an effective solution for multi-dimensional creative writing optimization.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "RLMR: Reinforcement Learning with Mixed Rewards for Creative Writing," along with a score and justification:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RLMR (Reinforcement Learning with Mixed Rewards), a novel framework for improving the performance of large language models (LLMs) in creative writing tasks. It addresses the challenge of balancing subjective writing quality (e.g., literariness, emotional expression) with objective constraint following (e.g., length limits, format requirements). RLMR uses a dynamically mixed reward system that combines a writing reward model (for subjective quality) with a constraint verification model (for objective adherence). The key innovation is the dynamic adjustment of the constraint-following reward weight based on the writing quality within sampled groups. This ensures that samples violating constraints receive a negative advantage during training, effectively penalizing them.  The method is evaluated across various model families and benchmarks and demonstrates improvements in both instruction following and writing quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Significance:** The paper addresses a real and important challenge in creative writing with LLMs. Balancing quality and constraints is crucial for practical applications.
    *   **Novelty:** The dynamic reward mixing strategy is a novel approach. It moves beyond fixed-weight combinations, which are often suboptimal.
    *   **Technical Soundness:** The method is well-explained, and the rationale behind the dynamic adjustment is clear. The mathematical formulation provides a formal grounding.
    *   **Experimental Rigor:** The paper includes comprehensive experiments across different model scales, families, and benchmarks, including a custom benchmark ("WriteEval"). Both automated and manual evaluations are conducted, which strengthen the findings.
    *   **Results:** The results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of RLMR compared to strong baselines, including single-reward and linearly weighted methods. The analysis of training dynamics provides additional insights into the method's behavior.
    *   **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and easy to understand. The figures and tables are helpful in presenting the results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** While GRPO is chosen for its computational efficiency, the dynamic reward adjustment mechanism likely adds some overhead compared to simpler approaches. A detailed analysis of computational cost would be beneficial.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The dynamic reward adjustment mechanism introduces hyperparameters (e.g., δ, γ). The paper could benefit from a discussion of the sensitivity of the results to these hyperparameters and how they were tuned.
    *   **Generalizability Beyond Creative Writing:** While the paper focuses on creative writing, the general idea of dynamically adjusting reward weights based on constraint satisfaction might be applicable to other tasks. A discussion of the potential for broader applications would be interesting.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of reinforcement learning for creative writing. It provides a practical and effective solution for a challenging problem.
    *   The dynamic reward mixing strategy could be a valuable technique for other RL tasks where balancing multiple objectives is important.
    *   The "WriteEval" benchmark could serve as a useful resource for future research in creative writing with LLMs.

**Score:** 8.5

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and effective approach to a significant problem in creative writing with LLMs. The technical soundness, experimental rigor, and clear presentation contribute to its high quality. While there are some minor weaknesses regarding computational cost and hyperparameter sensitivity, the overall contribution is substantial. The method's practical value and potential for broader applications justify a score of 8.5.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Beyond Benchmark: LLMs Evaluation with an Anthropomorphic and Value-oriented Roadmap
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.18646v1)
- **Authors**: Jun Wang, Ninglun Gu, Kailai Zhang, Zijiao Zhang, Yelun Bao, Jin Yang, Xu Yin, Liwei Liu, Yihuan Liu, Pengyong Li, Gary G. Yen, Junchi Yan
- **Abstract**: For Large Language Models (LLMs), a disconnect persists between benchmark performance and real-world utility. Current evaluation frameworks remain fragmented, prioritizing technical metrics while neglecting holistic assessment for deployment. This survey introduces an anthropomorphic evaluation paradigm through the lens of human intelligence, proposing a novel three-dimensional taxonomy: Intelligence Quotient (IQ)-General Intelligence for foundational capacity, Emotional Quotient (EQ)-Alignment Ability for value-based interactions, and Professional Quotient (PQ)-Professional Expertise for specialized proficiency. For practical value, we pioneer a Value-oriented Evaluation (VQ) framework assessing economic viability, social impact, ethical alignment, and environmental sustainability. Our modular architecture integrates six components with an implementation roadmap. Through analysis of 200+ benchmarks, we identify key challenges including dynamic assessment needs and interpretability gaps. It provides actionable guidance for developing LLMs that are technically proficient, contextually relevant, and ethically sound. We maintain a curated repository of open-source evaluation resources at: https://github.com/onejune2018/Awesome-LLM-Eval.
- **Summary**: This survey paper introduces an anthropomorphic and value-oriented roadmap for evaluating Large Language Models (LLMs). It proposes a three-dimensional taxonomy (IQ, EQ, PQ) to map LLM assessment to human cognitive progression and a Value-oriented Evaluation (VQ) framework to assess economic viability, social impact, ethical alignment, and environmental sustainability. The paper analyzes over 200 benchmarks, identifies challenges like dynamic assessment needs and interpretability gaps, and provides a roadmap for developing technically proficient, contextually relevant, and ethically sound LLMs. It includes a curated repository of evaluation resources. The paper also reviews existing LLM evaluation harnesses and proposes a modular LLM evaluation system.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its attempt to synthesize existing evaluation approaches into a more holistic and human-centric framework. The anthropomorphic perspective (IQ, EQ, PQ) is a conceptually interesting way to frame LLM evaluation, moving beyond purely technical metrics. The introduction of a VQ framework adds a layer of ethical and societal consideration often missing in LLM evaluations. However, the individual components (IQ, EQ, PQ metrics, value considerations) are not entirely new; rather, the paper's contribution is in their aggregation and structured presentation. The modular approach to evaluation harness design and discussion of prominent LLMs is well structured and helpful. While it provides the most prominent evaluation framework as of 2025 in the paper, it might become quickly outdated in the field, especially since the frameworks, models, metrics, and datasets grow and evolve rapidly. Also, the paper emphasizes mostly general frameworks and ignores recent approaches focused on safety evaluations of models. The VQ is promising, but the presented metrics are quite high-level and lack concrete methods for implementation.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial issue in the LLM field: the disconnect between benchmark performance and real-world utility. By emphasizing ethical considerations and societal impact (VQ), the authors highlight the importance of responsible AI development. The modular evaluation system and roadmap also offer practical guidance to researchers and developers. The detailed breakdown of evaluation metrics and existing tools is valuable. However, the true significance will depend on how widely the proposed framework is adopted and how effectively it can be translated into actionable evaluation practices. Also, the paper could provide a more in-depth explanation of various datasets that each of the proposed frameworks could use to give more concrete examples and instructions to the readers.

**Strengths:**

*   Holistic and human-centric approach.
*   Comprehensive overview of existing benchmarks and tools.
*   Practical roadmap for evaluation system design.
*   Emphasis on ethical and societal considerations (VQ).

**Weaknesses:**

*   VQ is high-level and lacks concrete implementation details, mostly dependent on the evaluation of others.
*   The components are somewhat incremental rather than groundbreaking.
*   The evaluation frameworks in the paper might get quickly outdated as time passes due to the rapid evolution of the field.
*   The anthropomorphic approach might oversimplify the complexities of both human and artificial intelligence.
*   Limited discussion on safety specific evaluation frameworks.

The survey's impact lies in its synthesis of existing ideas and its call for a more responsible and comprehensive approach to LLM evaluation. While not revolutionary, it offers a valuable framework for structuring future research and development efforts. The roadmap emphasizes how it can navigate the rapidly evolving landscape of AI assessment, but more robust metrics in each of the proposed evaluation frameworks will need to be put into place to create a more cohesive tool for analysis.

- **Score**: 7/10

### Thinking Before You Speak: A Proactive Test-time Scaling Approach
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.18648v1)
- **Authors**: Cong Li, Wenchang Chai, Hejun Wu, Yan Pan, Pengxu Wei, Liang Lin
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) often exhibit deficiencies with complex reasoning tasks, such as maths, which we attribute to the discrepancy between human reasoning patterns and those presented in the LLMs' training data. When dealing with complex problems, humans tend to think carefully before expressing solutions. However, they often do not articulate their inner thoughts, including their intentions and chosen methodologies. Consequently, critical insights essential for bridging reasoning steps may be absent in training data collected from human sources. To bridge this gap, we proposes inserting \emph{insight}s between consecutive reasoning steps, which review the status and initiate the next reasoning steps. Unlike prior prompting strategies that rely on a single or a workflow of static prompts to facilitate reasoning, \emph{insight}s are \emph{proactively} generated to guide reasoning processes. We implement our idea as a reasoning framework, named \emph{Thinking Before You Speak} (TBYS), and design a pipeline for automatically collecting and filtering in-context examples for the generation of \emph{insight}s, which alleviates human labeling efforts and fine-tuning overheads. Experiments on challenging mathematical datasets verify the effectiveness of TBYS. Project website: https://gitee.com/jswrt/TBYS
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Thinking Before You Speak: A Proactive Test-time Scaling Approach" introduces a novel prompting technique called "proactive prompting" for improving the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs), particularly in complex mathematical tasks. Unlike traditional prompting methods that rely on static prompts or pre-defined workflows, proactive prompting involves the LLM generating "insights" between reasoning steps. These insights review the current status and guide the next reasoning steps, mimicking human thought processes. The authors implement this idea as a framework called Thinking Before You Speak (TBYS). A pipeline is developed to automatically collect and filter in-context examples for generating insights, reducing manual effort. Experiments on mathematical datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of TBYS compared to standard methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core concept of proactive prompting – having the LLM generate its own guiding prompts (insights) *during* reasoning – is moderately novel. Existing chain-of-thought (CoT) and iterative prompting methods steer reasoning, but typically with *predefined* prompts or actions. TBYS distinguishes itself by the LLM actively generating these prompts. However, the idea of iterative refinement/reasoning with LLMs is not entirely new, so the novelty lies specifically in the proactive generation of insights. The automatic collection and filtering of insight examples is also a valuable, though incremental, contribution.

*   **Significance:** Improving the reasoning capabilities of LLMs, especially in challenging domains like mathematics, is a highly relevant and significant goal. TBYS addresses a crucial limitation of LLMs: the lack of explicit inner thoughts that humans use to guide reasoning. By forcing the LLM to articulate these thoughts as insights, TBYS produces more explainable reasoning traces, which may facilitate LLM learning and be more beneficial for human understanding. The performance improvements demonstrated on MATH-500 and AIME datasets, along with the improved accuracy-overhead trade-offs compared to baselines, suggest a tangible impact. However, the experiments are limited to two datasets and a single LLM, which somewhat limits the broader applicability of the findings. Further testing on other reasoning tasks and LLMs is required.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The proactive prompting paradigm is a conceptually appealing and potentially generalizable idea.
    *   The TBYS framework and the pipeline for automated insight collection are well-defined and implemented.
    *   Experimental results demonstrate tangible performance improvements over strong baselines on challenging mathematical tasks.
    *   Overhead analysis reveals better accuracy-overhead trade-offs compared to self-consistency.
    *   Qualitative analysis highlights how TBYS can effectively steer reasoning via insights.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Experimental evaluation is limited to two datasets and a single LLM (Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct).
    *   The effectiveness of TBYS heavily relies on the quality of the automatically collected insights, which may be sensitive to the choice of LLM, dataset, and filtering parameters.
    *   The paper does not thoroughly investigate the potential limitations of TBYS on different problem types or in scenarios where human-like insights may not be directly applicable.
    *   The computational overhead of TBYS remains a concern, despite the improved accuracy-overhead trade-off. Further optimizations may be necessary for practical applications.
    *   The library construction heavily uses CoT data, and it isn't perfectly clear how different this is from selecting useful CoT examples and then prompting for an "insight." While TBYS frames this as proactively generating prompts, there's a lot of reliance on what the LM already knows and can produce through simpler CoT methods.

*   **Potential Influence:** TBYS has the potential to influence the development of more robust and explainable reasoning techniques for LLMs. The proactive prompting paradigm could inspire new approaches to guide LLM reasoning in various domains. The automated insight collection pipeline may facilitate the creation of datasets and resources for training LLMs to generate more informative and helpful explanations.
    However, further research is needed to address the limitations mentioned above and to explore the broader applicability of TBYS.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper introduces a moderately novel proactive prompting paradigm and presents a well-defined TBYS framework with promising experimental results. The performance improvements and improved trade-offs versus Self-Consistency are real, but TBYS also adds a fair amount of complexity. It builds on strong CoT data and it isn't always clear how the *insight* generation is fundamentally different. While TBYS improves explainability, it comes at the cost of increased computational overhead, and performance may suffer from a suboptimal insight or insight library. This is also based on experimentation limited to two datasets and a single LM. The work has the potential to inspire new research directions in LLM reasoning, but its practical impact will depend on addressing the limitations and demonstrating broader applicability. Further experiments across diverse datasets and LLMs are required to establish its long-term significance.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### PRISM: Robust VLM Alignment with Principled Reasoning for Integrated Safety in Multimodality
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.18649v1)
- **Authors**: Nanxi Li, Zhengyue Zhao, Chaowei Xiao
- **Abstract**: Safeguarding vision-language models (VLMs) is a critical challenge, as existing methods often suffer from over-defense, which harms utility, or rely on shallow alignment, failing to detect complex threats that require deep reasoning. To this end, we introduce PRISM (Principled Reasoning for Integrated Safety in Multimodality), a system2-like framework that aligns VLMs by embedding a structured, safety-aware reasoning process. Our framework consists of two key components: PRISM-CoT, a dataset that teaches safety-aware chain-of-thought reasoning, and PRISM-DPO, generated via Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) to further refine this reasoning through Direct Preference Optimization to help obtain a delicate safety boundary. Comprehensive evaluations demonstrate PRISM's effectiveness, achieving remarkably low attack success rates including 0.15% on JailbreakV-28K for Qwen2-VL and 90% improvement over the previous best method on VLBreak for LLaVA-1.5. PRISM also exhibits strong robustness against adaptive attacks, significantly increasing computational costs for adversaries, and generalizes effectively to out-of-distribution challenges, reducing attack success rates to just 8.70% on the challenging multi-image MIS benchmark. Remarkably, this robust defense is achieved while preserving, and in some cases enhancing, model utility. To promote reproducibility, we have made our code, data, and model weights available at https://github.com/SaFoLab-WISC/PRISM.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "PRISM: Robust VLM Alignment with Principled Reasoning for Integrated Safety in Multimodality":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the critical challenge of safety alignment in Vision-Language Models (VLMs). Existing safety methods often suffer from over-defense (harming utility) or shallow alignment (failing to detect complex, reasoning-based threats).  The authors introduce PRISM (Principled Reasoning for Integrated Safety in Multimodality), a system2-like framework that aligns VLMs by embedding a structured, safety-aware reasoning process.  PRISM consists of two key components:

1.  **PRISM-CoT:** A dataset that teaches safety-aware chain-of-thought reasoning. The CoT process consists of Problem (identifying textual prompt issues), Caption (providing contextualized visual understanding), Reasoning (synthesizing multi-modal info), and Output (generating responses with safety justification).
2.  **PRISM-DPO:** A preference optimization dataset generated via Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) to refine reasoning through Direct Preference Optimization, helping to create a delicate safety boundary.

The authors demonstrate PRISM's effectiveness through comprehensive evaluations. The results show significantly reduced attack success rates, robustness against adaptive attacks, and effective generalization to out-of-distribution challenges while maintaining or even improving model utility. They release code, data, and model weights for reproducibility.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *structured reasoning framework*.  While chain-of-thought reasoning and DPO have been used in other contexts, the way PRISM applies and structures them specifically for multimodal safety, and leverages MCTS to generate the preference data, is a significant contribution. It's a more principled and explainable approach than many "black box" fine-tuning methods. The decomposition of multimodal safety into three categories with specialized COT also contributes to the novelty.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem in the VLM space: how to ensure models are safe *without* sacrificing utility.  Overly cautious models are less useful, while inadequately aligned models pose real-world risks.  PRISM offers a promising direction by embedding safety awareness directly into the model's reasoning process.  The performance gains on benchmarks like JailbreakV-28K, VLBreak, and MIS, coupled with the preservation of utility on MM-Vet-v2, demonstrate the practical impact.  The robustness against adaptive attacks is particularly important, indicating that PRISM isn't simply memorizing superficial patterns.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper thoroughly evaluates PRISM across several datasets and attack scenarios, demonstrating its broad effectiveness.
    *   **Principled Approach:**  The structured reasoning framework provides more explainability and control over the model's safety behavior.
    *   **Maintenance of Utility:** Unlike many safety alignment methods, PRISM demonstrably preserves (and sometimes enhances) the model's helpfulness.
    *   **Adaptive Attack Robustness:** Shows the defense isn't easily circumvented by iterative attacks.
    *   **Release of Resources:** The authors provide code, data, and models to promote reproducibility and further research.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependency on GPT-4:** The generation of the PRISM-CoT and evaluation on some tasks relies on GPT-4, which introduces potential biases and raises concerns about scalability and accessibility. While they do provide data, using a proprietary model to generate the training data makes the work less easily replicable outside a closed ecosystem.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The use of MCTS for preference data generation and the test-time scaling approach increase computational costs, which may be a barrier to adoption.
    *   **Limited Failure Case Analysis:** While they provide some failure analysis, a deeper dive into *why* certain attacks still succeed would be valuable. It would be beneficial to analyze the types of multimodal threats that still manage to circumvent the system.
*   **Potential Influence:** PRISM could influence future research by encouraging a shift toward more structured and explainable safety alignment methods. The chain-of-thought approach and preference optimization framework could be adapted and extended to address other challenges in VLM safety. The emphasis on balancing safety and utility is also important, as it recognizes the need for models that are both safe and useful. The identified categories of multimodal safety violations are also a valuable contribution.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a substantial contribution to VLM safety alignment by proposing a principled, structured reasoning framework (PRISM) that effectively balances safety and utility. The evaluations are thorough, demonstrating robustness against various attacks and strong generalization capabilities. While the reliance on GPT-4 and the increased computational costs are limitations, the strengths of the approach, the significance of the problem, and the potential for future influence outweigh these weaknesses.

- **Score**: 8/10

### Breaking the Trade-Off Between Faithfulness and Expressiveness for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.18651v1)
- **Authors**: Chenxu Yang, Qingyi Si, Zheng Lin
- **Abstract**: Grounding responses in external knowledge represents an effective strategy for mitigating hallucinations in Large Language Models (LLMs). However, current LLMs struggle to seamlessly integrate knowledge while simultaneously maintaining faithfulness (or fidelity) and expressiveness, capabilities that humans naturally possess. This limitation results in outputs that either lack support from external knowledge, thereby compromising faithfulness, or appear overly verbose and unnatural, thus sacrificing expressiveness. In this work, to break the trade-off between faithfulness and expressiveness, we propose Collaborative Decoding (CoDe), a novel approach that dynamically integrates output probabilities generated with and without external knowledge. This integration is guided by distribution divergence and model confidence, enabling the selective activation of relevant and reliable expressions from the model's internal parameters. Furthermore, we introduce a knowledge-aware reranking mechanism that prevents over-reliance on prior parametric knowledge while ensuring proper utilization of provided external information. Through comprehensive experiments, our plug-and-play CoDe framework demonstrates superior performance in enhancing faithfulness without compromising expressiveness across diverse LLMs and evaluation metrics, validating both its effectiveness and generalizability.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary of the paper, followed by a critical evaluation of its novelty and significance, and then assign a score with a justification.

**Summary:**

The paper "Breaking the Trade-Off Between Faithfulness and Expressiveness for Large Language Models" addresses a key challenge in retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems: the inherent trade-off between faithfulness to external knowledge and the expressiveness of the generated response. The authors argue that current LLMs either hallucinate or produce verbose, unnatural responses when incorporating external knowledge. They propose a novel decoding method called Collaborative Decoding (CoDe), which dynamically integrates output probabilities generated with and without external knowledge, guided by distribution divergence and model confidence. CoDe also includes a knowledge-aware reranking mechanism to prevent over-reliance on parametric knowledge. The authors present comprehensive experiments across various LLMs, datasets, and evaluation metrics to demonstrate that CoDe enhances faithfulness without compromising expressiveness. They claim that CoDe is a plug-and-play framework that doesn't require additional training, models, or generation budgets.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addressing an Important Problem:** The faithfulness-expressiveness trade-off is a well-recognized and significant problem in knowledge-augmented LLMs. The paper tackles this problem head-on.
    *   **Novel Approach:** The proposed CoDe method is a novel approach that combines distribution divergence, model confidence, and knowledge-aware reranking to achieve a dynamic integration of parametric and external knowledge. This is a fresh perspective compared to previous error-filtering approaches.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a comprehensive evaluation of CoDe across diverse LLMs, datasets, and a wide range of evaluation metrics (faithfulness, expressiveness, quality). This thoroughness strengthens the claims.
    *   **Practical Applicability:** The "plug-and-play" nature of CoDe makes it easily adaptable and deployable without requiring extensive training or model modifications.
    *   **Qualitative analysis:** The qualitative example shows how the proposed method addresses distinct failure modes where baselines fail.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While CoDe shows improvements over baselines, the gains might be considered incremental in some cases. The tables show that on some metrics, performance improvements is small. It would be interesting to have more detailed analysis of instances where CoDe fails.
    *   **Reliance on Existing Evaluation Metrics:** The paper relies heavily on automatic evaluation metrics, which are known to have limitations in capturing nuances of language and human judgment. While human evaluations are provided, a larger and more in-depth human study could further bolster the findings.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity Analysis:** Even though the authors mentioned a hyperparameter sensitivity analysis, it could be strengthened by providing specific guidelines on setting the hyperparameters for CoDe in different scenarios.

*   **Significance:**
    *   The paper contributes a valuable method (CoDe) to improve the performance of knowledge-augmented LLMs.
    *   The findings offer insights into the complex relationship between faithfulness and expressiveness in text generation.
    *   The CoDe framework can be used as a foundation for future research in decoding strategies for LLMs.

* **Novelty:**
    * The combination of distribution divergence, model confidence, and knowledge-aware reranking in a single decoding framework.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and practical solution to a significant problem in the field of knowledge-augmented LLMs. The comprehensive evaluation provides strong evidence for the effectiveness and generalizability of CoDe. However, the gains over baselines might be considered incremental, and there's reliance on automatic metrics. The plug-and-play nature and insightful findings still make it a significant contribution.

### Emotion Omni: Enabling Empathetic Speech Response Generation through Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.18655v1)
- **Authors**: Haoyu Wang, Guangyan Zhang, Jiale Chen, Jingyu Li, Yuehai Wang, Yiwen Guo
- **Abstract**: With the development of speech large language models (speech LLMs), users can now interact directly with assistants via speech. However, most existing models simply convert the response content into speech without fully understanding the rich emotional and paralinguistic cues embedded in the user's query. In many cases, the same sentence can have different meanings depending on the emotional expression. Furthermore, emotional understanding is essential for improving user experience in human-machine interaction. Currently, most speech LLMs with empathetic capabilities are trained on massive datasets. This approach requires vast amounts of data and significant computational resources. Therefore, a key challenge lies in how to develop a speech LLM capable of generating empathetic responses with limited data and without the need for large-scale training. To address this challenge, we propose Emotion Omni, a novel model architecture designed to understand the emotional content of user speech input and generate empathetic speech responses. Additionally, we developed a data generation pipeline based on an open-source TTS framework to construct a 200k emotional dialogue dataset, which supports the construction of an empathetic speech assistant. The demos are available at https://w311411.github.io/omni_demo/
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Emotion Omni: Enabling Empathetic Speech Response Generation Through Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Emotion Omni, a novel architecture for generating empathetic speech responses using large language models (LLMs). The key idea is to incorporate emotional features extracted from user speech input to control the emotional tone of the generated speech response. Emotion Omni employs separate speech and emotion encoders, a fusion module to combine semantic and emotional information, and adaptive layer normalization (AdaLN) for emotion style control.  The authors also present a low-cost data generation pipeline utilizing GPT-4o and CosyVoice2 to create a 200k emotional dialogue dataset. The paper evaluates the proposed model on various benchmarks and demonstrates its effectiveness in generating emotionally consistent responses.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Novelty:** The architecture introduces a novel approach to integrate emotional features into speech LLMs. Specifically, the emotion prediction head and AdaLN usage in speech decoding for emotion control presents a fresh approach compared to simple text-to-speech conversion based on LLMs. The use of GPT-4o and CosyVoice2 for data generation is an interesting and potentially impactful method for mitigating the lack of labeled emotional speech data.
    * **Significance:** Empathetic interaction is a crucial factor for improved user experience in conversational AI. This work addresses the need for speech LLMs that go beyond semantic understanding and consider the emotional context of the interaction.  The emphasis on a low-cost and scalable solution is important for enabling broader adoption of empathetic speech technology. The focus on a specific end-to-end architecture with an emphasis on efficient computation over reliance on purely large dataset training is valuable.
    * **Technical Soundness:** The approach is technically sound, combining existing components (e.g., Whisper, Emotion2Vec, Qwen2.5) in a novel way. The use of LoRA for efficient fine-tuning and the detailed architectural design are commendable.
    * **Empirical Evaluation:**  The paper includes a range of experiments, including benchmarks from VoiceBench, speech quality metrics (UTMOS), and empathetic response evaluation using GPT for scoring. This provides a reasonable assessment of the model's performance.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Novelty:** While the combination of different components is novel, the individual components themselves (Whisper, Emotion2Vec, Qwen2.5, AdaLN) are well-established. The contribution largely lies in the specific architecture and training process for combining these existing tools.
    * **Limited Evaluation on Complex Emotional Scenarios:** The evaluation could benefit from a deeper analysis of how the model handles nuanced emotional situations. A more detailed analysis of failure cases would highlight the strengths and weaknesses of the proposed approach. The data quality resulting from the synthesized dataset is not rigorously evaluated. It is possible that synthesized emotional labels do not map as neatly onto felt human emotions.
    * **Generalizability to Real-World Applications:** The focus on a controlled dataset might not fully capture the complexities of real-world interactions. The performance of Emotion Omni in noisy environments or with varying speech styles needs further investigation.
    * **Clarity on AdaLN's Contribution:** The paper mentions AdaLN, but the specific contribution of this technique relative to other parts of the architecture is not fully clarified. A more ablative study investigating various components of the proposed methodology would improve understanding.

* **Potential Influence:**
    * The paper provides a practical approach for building empathetic speech LLMs with relatively limited resources, which could be influential in enabling a wider range of applications.
    * The data generation pipeline could be adopted by other researchers to create emotional speech datasets.
    * The architecture may serve as a blueprint for future research on emotional speech understanding and generation.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and technically sound approach to generating empathetic speech responses using LLMs. The use of emotion encoders and AdaLN for emotion style control is a valuable contribution. However, the incremental nature of the novelty (combining existing components) and some limitations in the evaluation prevent it from receiving a higher score. The potential influence on the field is promising, but further research is needed to address the weaknesses.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Membership Inference Attacks on LLM-based Recommender Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.18665v1)
- **Authors**: Jiajie He, Yuechun Gu, Min-Chun Chen, Keke Chen
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) based Recommender Systems (RecSys) can flexibly adapt recommendation systems to different domains. It utilizes in-context learning (ICL), i.e., the prompts, to customize the recommendation functions, which include sensitive historical user-specific item interactions, e.g., implicit feedback like clicked items or explicit product reviews. Such private information may be exposed to novel privacy attack. However, no study has been done on this important issue. We design four membership inference attacks (MIAs), aiming to reveal whether victims' historical interactions have been used by system prompts. They are \emph{direct inquiry, hallucination, similarity, and poisoning attacks}, each of which utilizes the unique features of LLMs or RecSys. We have carefully evaluated them on three LLMs that have been used to develop ICL-LLM RecSys and two well-known RecSys benchmark datasets. The results confirm that the MIA threat on LLM RecSys is realistic: direct inquiry and poisoning attacks showing significantly high attack advantages. We have also analyzed the factors affecting these attacks, such as the number of shots in system prompts and the position of the victim in the shots.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates membership inference attacks (MIAs) on recommender systems (RecSys) that utilize large language models (LLMs) with in-context learning (ICL).  The authors propose four novel MIA methods: Direct Inquiry, Hallucination, Similarity, and Poisoning.  These attacks aim to determine if a user's interaction data was used to create the prompts that customize the LLM-based RecSys. They conduct experiments on three popular LLMs (Llama-2, Llama-3, Vicuna) and two benchmark datasets (MovieLens-1M, Amazon Digital Music).  The results show that Direct Inquiry and Poisoning attacks are effective, raising privacy concerns. They also analyze factors such as the number of shots in prompts and the position of attacked shots.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its focus on MIAs *specifically targeting* LLM-based recommender systems using in-context learning.  While MIAs have been studied in other contexts (traditional RecSys, general machine learning), the application to this *new* architecture presents unique challenges and opportunities. The design of the four proposed attacks leverages unique characteristics of LLMs, such as memorization and hallucination. However, the "Similarity" attack is essentially an adaptation of existing techniques from traditional RecSys MIAs. Therefore, this limits the overall novelty.

*   **Significance:** The work is significant because LLMs are increasingly being used in recommender systems, raising new privacy concerns.  Demonstrating that user data can be leaked through prompts, even without access to model internals, is a valuable contribution. This work highlights potential vulnerabilities that RecSys developers need to address. It provides early insights for designing prompts and model architectures that are more resilient to privacy attacks. Furthermore, the analysis of factors influencing attack success provides practical guidance. It opens a new avenue of research into privacy issues surrounding LLM-based RecSys and will spur further investigations.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clearly Defined Problem:** The paper clearly identifies the problem of privacy leakage in LLM-based RecSys with ICL.
    *   **Novel Attack Vectors:** The Direct Inquiry and Poisoning attacks are well-motivated and leverage specific LLM properties.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experiments are extensive, covering multiple LLMs, datasets, and attack parameters.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The analysis of factors affecting attack success has direct implications for RecSys design.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Generalizability of "Similarity" Attack:** The Similarity attack is largely a replication of previous work, and its failure highlights the differences between item embedding and text embedding.
    *   **Attack Reliance on Strong Assumptions:** While the black-box setting is reasonable, the reliance on knowing the target user's historical interactions is still a relatively strong assumption. However, this is a common assumption in MIA studies.
    *   **Missing Mitigation Strategies:** The paper mainly focuses on demonstrating the existence of the problem and does not propose concrete mitigation strategies to defend against MIAs. This is reasonable given the exploratory nature of the work but limits its immediate practical impact.

*   **Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a reasonably significant contribution by highlighting previously unexplored privacy challenges posed by the integration of LLMs into recommender systems through ICL. The Direct Inquiry and Poisoning attacks demonstrate real vulnerabilities. The research could be stronger with more in-depth analysis of the specific types of data leakage, and inclusion of preliminary defense strategies. However, this is a good first step in an important area. The overall impact will depend on whether this work spurs further research and the development of effective privacy protection mechanisms.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Optimal Sparsity of Mixture-of-Experts Language Models for Reasoning Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.18672v1)
- **Authors**: Taishi Nakamura, Satoki Ishikawa, Masaki Kawamura, Takumi Okamoto, Daisuke Nohara, Jun Suzuki, Rio Yokota
- **Abstract**: Empirical scaling laws have driven the evolution of large language models (LLMs), yet their coefficients shift whenever the model architecture or data pipeline changes. Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models, now standard in state-of-the-art systems, introduce a new sparsity dimension that current dense-model frontiers overlook. We investigate how MoE sparsity influences two distinct capability regimes: memorization and reasoning. We train families of MoE Transformers that systematically vary total parameters, active parameters, and top-$k$ routing while holding the compute budget fixed. For every model we record pre-training loss, downstream task loss, and task accuracy, allowing us to separate the train-test generalization gap from the loss-accuracy gap. Memorization benchmarks improve monotonically with total parameters, mirroring training loss. By contrast, reasoning performance saturates and can even regress despite continued gains in both total parameters and training loss. Altering top-$k$ alone has little effect when active parameters are constant, and classic hyperparameters such as learning rate and initialization modulate the generalization gap in the same direction as sparsity. Neither post-training reinforcement learning (GRPO) nor extra test-time compute rescues the reasoning deficit of overly sparse models. Our model checkpoints, code and logs are open-source at https://github.com/rioyokotalab/optimal-sparsity.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the optimal sparsity of Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) language models, specifically examining how sparsity affects memorization and reasoning tasks. The authors train families of MoE Transformers with varying total parameters, active parameters, and top-k routing, while holding the compute budget constant. They analyze pre-training loss, downstream task loss, and task accuracy to disentangle generalization and loss-accuracy gaps.  The key findings are that while increasing total parameters (and hence sparsity) generally improves memorization, reasoning performance plateaus and even degrades. The optimal sparsity level depends on the task and compute budget, and it's not rescued by post-training RL (GRPO) or increased test-time compute. They also find that the classic hyper-parameters, like learning rate and initialization, also play important role that correlates with sparsity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its systematic exploration of MoE sparsity's impact on reasoning *compared to* memorization tasks, combined with the effort to disentangle various performance gaps (generalization, loss-accuracy). Prior work often focused on scaling laws in general or specific architectural features. Here the authors specifically investigate sparsity and top-k in the MoE architecture with controlled compute, which is novel. The observation that simply increasing total parameters (leading to higher sparsity) can *hurt* reasoning despite continued improvements in pre-training loss is significant. The exploration of test-time compute and RL on this problem is also a valuable aspect, demonstrating that the core issue is not simply a matter of insufficient training or inference.

*   **Significance:** MoE models are becoming increasingly important.  Understanding their optimal configuration, especially with respect to different types of tasks, has substantial practical implications. The work provides valuable insights for architects of large language models. The observation about the importance of balancing active and total parameters, and the interplay between sparsity, hyperparameter tuning, and reasoning performance has useful practical guidance, especially in resource constrained setups. By open sourcing their mode checkpoints, code and logs, they are contributing to transparency and reproducibility.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Systematic Experimentation:**  The paper's strength is its rigorous and controlled experimentation. The authors carefully vary parameters and measure relevant metrics.
    *   **Disentanglement of Performance Gaps:**  The authors made considerable effort to distinguish between the generalization gap (train vs test loss), the loss-accuracy gap, and the impact of sparsity.
    *   **Focus on Reasoning:**  The explicit focus on reasoning tasks, where scaling laws are less well-understood than memorization, is important.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The incorporation of GRPO and test-time compute analysis adds depth to the findings.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dataset Size:** Although the 125B token dataset is a large amount, it's smaller than the data commonly used to train large MoEs, which can limit the strength and generalizability of conclusions.
    *   **Model Backbone:** Restricting their exploration to the Mixtral backbone while beneficial for comparison and isolation, might not generalize to other MoE architectures or gating strategies.
    *   **Task Specificity:** While investigating memorization and reasoning task separately, tasks are also very specific. The definition of reasoning task may be limited.
